115 年 8 月海關進出口貿易初步統計 
一、進出口貿易變動 
（一）出口 
8 月出口 824.0 億美元，創歷年單月新高，較上月增 9.4%，較上年
同月增 41.0%(按新臺幣計算增 52.6%)；累計 1 至 8 月出口 5,743.4
億美元，較上年同期增 44.2%(按新臺幣計算增 46.5%)。 
（二）進口 
8 月進口 601.0 億美元，為歷年單月第 3 高，較上月增 3.4%，較上
年同月增 44.3%(按新臺幣計算增 56.2%)；累 計1 至 8 月進口 4,374.3
億美元，較上年同期增 40.4%(按新臺幣計算增 42.4%)。 
（三）出入超 
8 月出超 223.0 億美元，為單月新高，較上年同月增 55.2 億美元；
累計 1 至 8 月出超 1,369.1 億美元，較上年同期增 500.5 億美元。

### Page 2

2 
二、出口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
受惠於人工智慧(AI)、 高效能運算及雲端服務等應用持續擴增，相關
產品出貨維持強勁， 加上價格攀升效 應，8月出口較上年同月增 239.7
億美元(+41.0%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 電子零組件出口 322.2 億美元(歷年單月新高)， 較上年同月增118.3
億美元(+58.0%)，其中積體電路增 115.5 億美元(+60.0%)、印刷電
路增 1.4 億美元(+29.9%)、 電容器及電阻器增0.7 億美元(+24.9%)。 
2. 資通與視聽產品出口 331.0 億美元，較上年同月增 97.6 億美元
(+41.8%)，其中電腦及其附屬單元增68.7 億美元(+40.4%)、儲存媒
體增 19.1 億美元(+2.4 倍)、 交換器及路由器增9.7 億美元(+1.1 倍)。 
3. 礦產品出口 20.0 億美元，較上年同月增 8.3 億美元(+70.2%)，其
中石油煉製品增 7.8 億美元(+74.3%)。 
4. 基本金屬及其製品出口 29.6 億美元，較上年同月增 5.4 億美元
(+22.4%)，其中銅及其製品增 6.1 億美元(+1.5 倍)，鋼鐵及其製品
減 1.1 億美元(-8.7%)。 
5. 機械出口 26.8 億美元，較上年同月增 3.6 億美元(+15.2%)，其中
生產半導體等機械增 1.9 億美元(+42.1%)、未列名具特殊功能機械
增 0.4 億美元(+39.0%)。

（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 8 月出口較上年同期增 1,759.8 億美元(+44.2%)，以資通與
視聽產品增 1,023.7 億美元(+68.5%)、電子零組件增 629.5 億美元
(+45.8%)增加較多。

生產等出現結構性轉變，爰另予併計。

三、進口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
由於 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口衍生需求之帶動，積體電路及資通產
品進口維持強勁，加上輸入物價明顯上漲，8 月總進口較上年同月增
184.5 億美元(+44.3%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 農工原料進口408.4 億美元， 較上年同月增129.4億美元(+46.4%)，
其中電子零組件增 82.5 億美元(+62.6%)、礦產品增 16.9 億美元
(+38.4%)、電腦之零附件增 14.6 億美元(+84.4%)、基本金屬增 4.5
億美元(+21.8%)、塑化原料增 4.0 億美元(+16.2%)。 
2. 資本設備進口 130.6 億美元， 較上年同月增 38.4 億美元(+41.6%)，
其中資通設備增 27.2 億美元(+1.2 倍)、半導體設備增 2.8 億美元
(+7.3%)、交通運輸設備增 2.8 億美元(+1.1 倍)。 
3. 消費品進口 55.7 億美元，較上年同月增 16.0 億美元(+40.5%)，其
中固態非揮發性儲存裝置增12.1 億美元(+3.7 倍)、藥粧用品增 1.2
億美元(+17.4%)、手機增 1.1 億美元(+78.1%)。

四、主要出口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
8 月對陸港、日本出口創新高，對美國、東協為單月第 3 高 
1. 對美國出口 236.7 億美元，較上年同月增 40.6 億美元(+20.7%)，
其中資通與視聽產品增 23.0 億美元(+14.6%)、電子零組件增 11.6
億美元(+1.5 倍)、電機產品增 1.7 億美元(+30.1%)。 
2. 對中國大陸與香港出口 211.5 億美元，較上年同月增 59.7 億美元
(+39.3%)，其中電子零組件增 47.4 億美元(+45.6%)、資通與視聽
產品增 6.7 億美元(+42.9%)、 基本金屬及其製品增4.6 億美元(+1.2
倍)。 
3. 對東協出口 151.3 億美元，較上年同月增 44.9 億美元(+42.2%)，
其中電子零組件增 25.3 億美元(+50.3%)、資通與視聽產品增 12.6
億美元(+41.1%)、礦產品增 4.6 億美元(+1.5 倍)。 
4. 對歐洲出口 44.5 億美元，較上年同月增 11.5 億美元(+34.8%)，其
中資通與視聽產品增 6.6 億美元(+57.0%)、電子零組件增 4.0 億美
元(+1.1 倍)，基本金屬及其製品減 1.4 億美元(-31.0%)。 
5. 對日本出口 36.6 億美元，較上年同月增8.9 億美元(+32.0%)，其中
電子零組件增 8.9 億美元(+76.7%)、 化學品增0.5 億美元(+32.3%)，
資通與視聽產品減 0.6 億美元(-13.9%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 8 月對美國出口 1,803.2 億美元，較上年同期增 631.6 億美
元(+53.9%)，對中國大陸與香港出口 1,394.7 億美元，增 307.5 億美
元(+28.3%)， 對東協出口1,150.7 億美元 ， 增372.3 億美元(+47.8%)，
對歐洲出口 376.6 億美元 ， 增130.7 億美元(+53.1%)， 對日本出口247.0
億美元，增 54.7 億美元(+28.4%)。

五、主要進口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
8 月自中國大陸與香港進口 126.8 億美元 ， 較上年同月增47.6 億美元
(+60.2%)，自東協進口 106.9 億美元，增46.7 億美元(+77.6%)，自南
韓進口 91.7 億美元，增29.6 億美元(+47.7%)，自日本進口 57.0 億美
元，增 11.2 億美元(+24.4%)，自美國進口 53.9 億美元，增 14.3 億美
元(+35.9%)，自歐洲進口 49.5 億美元，增 6.2 億美元(+14.3%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 8 月自主要國家/地區進口與上年同期比較 ， 自東協增75.4%、
自南韓增 63.2%、自中國大陸與香港增 40.5%、自美國增 24.4%、自
日本增 24.1%、自歐洲增 12.8%。

六、主要國家/地區貿易概況 
（一）今年 1-7 月主要國家/地區外貿景氣普遍上升，南韓(1-8 月)、香港、
新加坡出口動能尤為強勁，分別年增 52.8%、46.6%、38.3%；中國
大陸(1-8 月)、美國出口分別年增 19.3%及 14.6%，德國(1-6 月)、日
本各年增 10.9%、8.1%。 
（二）主要國家/地區進口同步上揚，累計 1-7 月以香港進口年增 48.1%最
為顯著， 新加坡、 中國大陸(1-8 月)分別年增 37.0%、27.0%，南韓(1-8
月)、德國(1-6 月)各增 18.7%、11.7%，日本、美國各增 6.5%、1.2%。

圖 5 2026 年主要國家/地區出進口年增率
七、綜合分析
（一）得益於人工智慧(AI)、高效能運算及 雲端服務等應用 需求強勁， 加
以外銷產品價格攀升，8 月出口 824.0 億美元，創歷年單月新高，年
增 41.0%，連續 34 個月正成長；進口 601.0 億美元，年增 44.3%，
主因 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口衍生需求擴張 ，及農工原料行情上
漲；出、進口互抵後，出超 223.0 億美元，亦創單月新高 。累計 1
至 8 月出口年增 44.2%、進口增 40.4%。
（二）8 月出口主要貨品以電子零組件、資通與視聽產品貢獻最多，年增率
分別為 58.0%及 41.8%，前者受惠 AI 商機及記憶體外銷價量齊揚，規
模值創單月新高，兩類併計增 49.3%，其餘貨類平均增 16.3%，以礦
產品隨油品售價上漲，年增 70.2%較為突出，基本金屬及其製品、電
機產品各增 22.4%、22.3%。累計 1 至 8 月資通與視聽產品出口值已
超越歷年水準，與電子零組件合計占總出口比重達 78.7%。

（三）8 月對主要市場出口同呈雙位數成長，其中對中國大陸與香港、日本
受電子零組件外銷強勁推升，出口值同創單月新高，各年增 39.3%、
32.0%，對東協、歐洲、美國分別增 42.2%、34.8%、20.7%；五大市
場之外，對墨西哥及澳大利亞出口皆增逾 2 倍。累計 1 至 8 月對美
國、歐洲出口各年增 53.9%、53.1%，對東協年增 47.8%，對日本、
陸港各增 28.4%、28.3%，對五大市場出口規模均為歷年同期最佳。
（四）展望未來，地緣政治衝突 及美國關稅政策變化等不確定 因素，持續
干擾全球經濟成長力道，惟隨人工智慧應用 不斷深化，算力需求攀
升，國際雲端服務業者加速建置 AI 基礎設施，加以國內半導體及伺
服器等供應鏈具競爭優勢，均有助支撐我國外銷動能，下半年出口
可望維持成長態勢。

Growth in multiples
We forecast revenue to more than double next year
Bloom Energy’s planned capacity expansion of up to 10GW strengthens Kaori’s
long-term SOFC growth outlook, while higher liquid cooling content and
shipments should sustain liquid cooling momentum into 2027. CSP CDU heat
exchangers and semiconductor equipment cooling could provide additional

growth drivers from 4Q26. We raise our 2027/28 EPS forecasts by 14%/12% and
increase our target price to NT$2,710 from NT$2,300. Reiterate HC O-PF rating.
Bloom Energy to double its capacity
Bloom Energy (BE) has announced plans to acquire a 158k sqft facility in F remont,
U
S, comparable in size to its existing Fremont manufacturing plant, which can
produce up to 5GW. We believe the acquisition could support an eventual doubling
of its fuel cell stack manufacturing capacity to 10GW. We expect its supply chain

to support shipments of 2/4/6GW in 2026/27/28 to meet strong solid oxide fuel
cell (SOFC). demand. To support this ramp, Kaori is expanding capacity to 6-7x its
current level by end-2027. We now forecast Kaori’s sales from BE to grow by
185%/66% YoY in 2027/28, versus our previous estimate of 101%/77%.
Strong liquid cooling momentum into 2027
With SpaceX planning an additional 6-8GW of datacentre capacity in 2027, we
b
elieve GPU server suppliers Supermicro and Dell will be key beneficiaries,

supporting continued growth across their supply chains from 2H26 into 2027.
Higher content value for Rubin manifolds, together with stronger CDU and rear-
door heat exchanger (RDHx) shipments, should support Kaori’s liquid cooling
momentum. We forecast its liquid cooling revenue to grow by 60%/25% YoY in
2027/28.
Diversifying the growth engines
While SOFC and Supermicro-related liquid cooling remain Kaori’s main growth
d
rivers, we believe plate heat exchangers for CSP CDUs and liquid cooling

applications in semiconductor equipment could emerge as additional growth drivers
from 4Q26. These opportunities should broaden Kaori’s customer and application
exposure, supporting its longer-term growth outlook.
Earnings revisions and valuation
We raise our 2027/28SP EPS forecasts by 14%/12% to reflect higher expected
d
emand and shipments for BE's fuel cells, alongside a stronger liquid cooling
outlook. We increase our TP to NT$2,710 from NT$2,300, based on an unchanged

40x 2H27-1H28SP PE. Reiterate High Conviction Outperform rating.

Investment thesis
Kaori is well positioned to capitalise on rising demand for liquid cooling, and it
expects related product shipments to ramp up. The fuel-cell business should be a
huge growth driver in 2026-27 to meet AI power infrastructure demand in the US.
In the longer term, we believe the company will also benefit from heat-pump
demand as Europe is phasing out gas boilers, despite some noise about near-term
demand. Kaori has been planning capacity expansion earlier than peers to fully

capture demand, with rising exposure to large clients. We thus expect the company
to enjoy robust earnings in coming years.

### Page 5

Growth in multiples Kaori - HC O-PF
6 October 2026 5
Catalysts
The major growth catalysts largely depend on fuel-cell projects for AI datacentres
taking off, increasing content value in liquid cooling offerings, and a clearer outlook
for hydrogen electrolyzer development.
Valuation details
We adopt a PE-based valuation methodology as we believe the market will mainly
focus on the earnings growth outlook, which is better reflected with PE metrics.

We base our valuation on 40x PE, which is around +1sd of its five-year average and
supported by an 93% EPS Cagr in 2026-28SP, to account for the surging demand
for AI server liquid cooling and power infrastructure. We are positive on the
company's long-term outlook given its growing stationary fuel cell demand from AI
datacentres, as well as its efforts to diversify customer exposure for liquid cooling
business. We believe the company can maintain its competitive edge over other

cooling players given its expertise in vacuum welding technology to manufacture
high-quality products.
Investment risks
Downside risks: 1) increased competition for Bloom Energy's fuel cell business or
new technology solutions replacing fuel cells; 2) slower kick-off of new fuel cell
projects for AI datacentres; 3) weaker-than-expected liquid cooling adoption; 4)
slower rollout of Europe/US government subsidies for heat pump installations.

### Page 1

www.imeritz.com

삼성전자 (005930)

Company Brief 
반도체/디스플레이 
Analyst 김선우 
sunwoo.kim@meritz.co.kr 
RA 우서현 
seohyun.woo@meritz.co.kr 2027년 메모리 판가·실적 상향 조정  
3Q26E 영업이익 106.1조원, 원화강세 불구 견조한 수준 기록 전망 
삼성전자의 3Q26 영업이익은 106.1조원으로 낮아진 시장 기대치 (104조원)를 소폭 
능가할 전망이다. 해당 분기동안 급격한 원화강세로 인해 실적 측면의 비우호적 환경
이 조성되었지만, 동사는 강건한 판매 정책에 기반해 메모리 판가가 산업 평균을 재
차 능가하며 견조한 실적 개선이 가능했다. 
동사 메모리 사업부의 3Q26 영업이익은 109조원으로 예상되며, 이중 DRAM과 
NAND는 각각 80조원 (OPM 78.5%) 과 29조원 (OPM 68.9%) 을 차지할 전망이다. 
DRAM과 NAND 출하량은 기존 예상치를 소폭 하회하는 +3.4%/+4.2% QoQ을 기
록하겠지만, 판가가 무려 +20.1%/19.0% QoQ 상승하며 실적 개선을 이끌겠다. 전
분기에 이어 ‘DS 특별경영성과급’ 충당금이 반영되며 손익에 일부 영향을 미치겠지
만, 동사 특유의 ‘수익성 극대화’ 판가 정책이 산업 우위 구도를 조성한다. 한편 LSI/
파운드리는 영업손실이 1.5조원을 기록하며 적자가 지속될 전망이다. 가동률 개선에 
기반한 사업 회복이 진행중이지만, 테일러팹 가동과 함께 비용 요인이 지속 출회할 
전망이다. 한편 반도체 외 기타 사업부의 험난한 실적 추이는 지속될 전망이다. SDC 
1.1조원 영업이익을 제외하고, DX (세트) 부문의 경우 MX -1.9조원, DA/VD -7,400
억원으로 영업적자 확대되리라 예상된다. 당사가 지난해 말부터 주장했듯, 세트 사업
은 메모리 등 부품원가 비중이 급격히 상승하는 가운데 향후 ‘판가 인상 → 판매량 
축소’의 ‘내구재화’ 변모 속 적자 최소화 노력만이 불가피할 전망이다.

1Q27 일부 모바일 DRAM 판가 +50% 폭등 예상. 적극적 매수 권고 
AGI를 향한 경쟁적이며 구조적 서버 수요 증가 불구, 스마트폰 중심의 B2C 응용처
는 유일한 판가저항처로 남아있다. 하지만 당사의 채널체크에 따르면, 메모리 업체들
의 지속적인 생산 전환 (LPDDR → 서버 DRAM 및 HBM)에 기반해 주요 고객 향 
모바일 메모리 판가는 무려 1Q27부터 50% 이상 QoQ 상향될 전망이다. 주요 고객
은 내년 20주년 플래그십 스마트폰을 제작하는 상황에서 판가 폭등을 감내해야하는 
상황이다. 최근 메모리 사이클 개선 속도에 대한 막연하며 진부한 우려가 존재하나, 
향후 1~2개월간 메모리 업체들에 대한 2027년 실적 추정치 상향과 함께 ‘성장·저평
가·주주환원 매력’이 동시에 발생할 전망이다. 동사의 2027년 영업이익 추정치를 기
존의 562조원에서 632조원으로 13% 상향조정하며, 시장 눈높이 역시 지속 상향되
리라 예상한다. 한편 배당 중심으로 발표됐던 동사의 주주환원의 경우, 모든 주주에게 
더 실효적인 ‘자사주 매입·소각’ 방안이 병행되는 방향으로 변모하리라 예상된다. 투
자의견 Buy와 적정주가 50만원을 유지한다. 
 Meritz Research  2026. 10. 7

Buy (20거래일 평균종가 대비 상승 여력 기준)

Document 149: AI Adoption Tracker_ August 2026_ Adoption Rises to 22.4%

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI Adoption Tracker_ August 2026_ Adoption Rises to 22.4%.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-09-01
Pages: 24
Status: ok

### Page 1

We update our AI adoption tracker through August 2026. n
AI-related investment growth remains strong, particularly for semiconductors, n
where equity analysts expect global revenue to reach $863bn (ann.) by the end 
of 2026. 2026 gross profit forecast revisions since 2022Q3 for AI-exposed global 
public companies currently stands at $1.1tn. AI-related hardware investment in 
the US national accounts also continues to rise and now stands at $485bn 
(3-month annualized average; 1.5% of GDP) above its 2022 level. AI-related 
hardware shipments from Taiwan have grown by 76% since 2022 and US AI 
hardware net imports ticked up to $55.9bn in June.  
AI adoption by firms rose by 0.9pp to 22.4% among US establishments (with n
adoption expected to rise to 25.9% in the next 6 months) according to the 
Census Bureau’s Business Trends and Outlook Survey (BTOS). Information is 
leading sector adoption at nearly 50%. At a detailed subsector level, computing 
and publishing firms continue to lead adoption, and warehousing and 
miscellaneous manufacturing firms report the largest expected increase in 
adoption over the next six months. Establishments with over 250 employees 
continue to lead in adoption (40%; latest six survey average) while adoption has 
grown more slowly recently among smaller firms. Other business surveys note 
that 40% of surveyed organizations have begun deploying and scaling AI agents. 
AI’s labor market impact remains visible but narrow. We continue to observe n
employment drags in specific industries where AI-use cases have been 
established such as marketing, graphic design, customer service, and some tech 
occupations. However, this is offset by construction job growth in data 
center-exposed categories, which is up 209k since 2022 (relative to broader 
construction employment trends). The tech share of total employment continues 
to decline relative to the pre-2022 trend. The correlation between AI adoption 
and broad labor market slack measures remains limited but has strengthened in 
recent months. Corporate layoffs attributed to AI affected 11k employees in 
June, bringing the 2026 YTD total to 113k. 22% of Russell 3000 companies 
mentioned AI and labor-related keywords in 2026Q2. 
We continue to observe large impacts on labor productivity in the limited areas n
where generative AI has been deployed but only modest signs of economy-wide 
impacts. Academic studies imply a 23% average uplift to productivity, while 
company anecdotes imply slightly larger efficiency gains of around 32%.

starting from the December 4, 2025 release. There was a level shift observed in 
conjunction with the new question wording, and we will be tracking adoption rates 
under the new methodology going forward.

### Page 11

Exhibit 15: 40% of Surveyed Organizations Have Begun Deploying and Scaling AI Agents 
Source Date Current Adoption / Impacts Future Adoption / Impacts
McKinsey August 2026
40% of large organization respondents have adopted AI agents and are 
scaling their deployment throughout the organization, up from 27% last year. 
Nearly a third of respondents reported that they had used agentic coding 
tools to build software in-house instead of purchasing it. 80% of respondents 
said AI has improved their individual productivity. 
60% of surveyed organizations expect to increase their AI investments in the 
year ahead. 39% of respondents expect AI-related declines in their 
organization's total employment in the coming year. 
Gallup July 2026
47% of surveyed US employees in Q2 said their organization has integrated 
AI tools to improve workplace productivity, up from 41% last quarter. 
Individual AI use at work also increased to 52% of respondents. Among AI 
users, the most common uses of AI are writing and editing (51%), search or 
research (49%), and general assistance or problem-solving (39%).
The highest productivity ratings come from employees using AI for coding 
assistance and automation or process automation. Employees who use AI 
for a greater variety of tasks were also  more likely to report that AI positively 
affects their personal productivity. Although corporate adoption and AI 
awareness has grown notably, one in five employees remain unsure if AI has 
been integrated at their organization
WRITER April 2026
52% of employees say they've used AI agents over the past year. 
Employees reported saving around 6 hours per week with AI tools, while 
executives reported saving twice as much time at nearly 12 hours per week. 
The top use cases include editing and proofreading, personalized 
communication, and data analysis and insights. Few surveyed leaders 
reported seeing significant ROI from generative AI (29%) or AI agents (23%).
69% of executives report doing layoffs due to AI, but 39% admit their 
company doesn't have a formal strategy to drive revenue from AI. 
Harvard Business 
Review and 
Appian
April 2026
Most organizations have moved beyond the experimentation phase with AI 
and 58% of surveyed organizations have AI in production. 69% of 
respondents reported that legacy systems are limiting their organization's 
ability to scale AI. Most respondents are seeing some returns from AI 
investments, but only 16% report realizing a high degree of measurable 
value. 
Many organizations are currently exploring agentic AI systems (under 
consideration by 62% of respondents and currently used by 25%). Although 
nearly all organizations agree that clear guardrails are needed for AI agent 
adoption, fewer than half say their organization has defined such rules.
Gallup April 2026
Half of all surveyed adults reported using AI in their jobs and 27% of 
employees in AI-adopting organizations say that their workplace has 
changed in disruptive ways due to AI in the past year. 65% of adopters said 
that AI has improved their productivity and efficiency, regardless of how often 
they personally use AI at work. 
Eighteen percent of all U.S. employees say it is very or somewhat likely their 
job will be eliminated within the next five years due to AI or automation. 
Among employees working in organizations that have adopted AI, that share 
rises to 23%. However, employees in AI-adopting organizations were more 
likely to report both current expansions and reductions in headcount. 
NVIDIA March 2026
64% of respondents said their organizations are actively using AI in their 
operations. 28% said they're still in the assessment phase, while 8% said 
they're not using AI and have no plans to. Regional adoption among 
respondents was 70% in North America, 65% in EMEA, and 63% in APAC. 
Larger companies (with over 1000 employees) demonstrated broader 
adoption, deployed more use cases, and reported greater ROI. More than 
half of respondents (53%) said improved employee productivity was one of 
the biggest impacts AI had on business operations.
86% of respondents said their AI budgets would increase this year and 
nearly 40% said budgets would increase by 10% or more. Respondents 
reported that workflow and operations and retaining the right expertise to 
scale AI solutions were the key challenges to adoption. 
Deloitte January 2026
In the latest survey, 34% of companies were starting to use AI to deeply 
transform their businesses, 30% were designing key processes around AI, 
and the remaining 37% were using AI at the surface level with little or no 
change to underlying business processes. 84% of companies have not 
redesignated jobs or work tasks around AI capabilities. 25% of surveyed 
leaders reported that AI is having a transformative effect on their 
organizations, more than double from 12% a year ago. 
Survey results showed that insufficient employee skills were the largest 
barrier to integrating AI into existing workflows. Nearly three-quarters of 
surveyed companies expect to be using agentic AI at least moderately in two 
years. 
Gallup December 2025
The percentage of US employees who reported using AI at work at least a 
few times a week increased from 19% in 2025Q2 to 23% in Q3. 
Unsurprisingly, employees working in knowledge-based jobs (such as tech 
or professional services) were more likely to use AI than those in frontline 
positions. Most common use cases include using AI to consolidate 
information or data, idea generation, and automating basic tasks. Users 
most often use AI chatbots and virtual assistants. 
Gallup research has shown that employee AI adoption is strongly associated 
with having greater managerial support for AI and strategic integration of AI 
in their role, indicating that a strategic people-first approach is needed for 
effective AI adoption.

Green shading indicates surveys added since the last update.

### Page 3

28 September 2026
3
Summary
Executive
• Summer brought little of the calm markets had priced in before it began. Three
tensions we had been tracking escalated through the season, surfacing as fresh
flashpoints by early September: a fresh energy shock out of the Middle East, a bond
sell-off that has spread from the US to Japan to Europe (and drawn erratic policy
responses from central banks and governments alike) and renewed doubts about the AI
boom – talk of chip and power shortages, bubble concerns and underwhelming returns

on frontier-model spend.
• But none of this has yet broken the real economy. Q2 growth surprised to the
upside, carried by net exports and Germany‘s stimulus in Europe, and by robust AI-
related investment in the US and Asia. Forward-looking indicators such as European
manufacturing PMIs at four-year highs and strong US Q3 nowcasts show little sign of
deterioration, albeit with marked divergence across sectors.
• The Great Unwind: the case for an orderly path forward. Our baseline continues to

be that these three tension points will ease rather than escalate, but that call rests
on specific mechanisms. In the report we also model three downside scenarios and
their economic and capital market impacts, each based on a disorderly release of the
tensions.
o The energy shock risk. We expect the conflict to stay contained and that
rerouting, demand destruction and alternative supply do the job energy markets
need them to do, easing prices and letting the inflation shock fade. This rests on

the assumption that the Saudi pipeline outage does not extend past a month. If
the outage runs longer, the shortfall could exceed 8Mb/d and Brent could stay
near USD100/bbl, turning what is now a contained shock into a stagflationary
shock.
o The bond sell-off risk. We assume inflation moderates without meaningful
second-round wage effects, and that sovereign markets can absorb elevated
issuance without a disorderly rise in risk premia.  The assumption doing the real

work here is political: the US midterms don‘t produce further fiscal slippage or
erratic policy interventions and European elections (France, Italy) do not add fiscal
uncertainty. Note that the real rate component of the US 10 treasury rates has
increased by a full percentage point this past year confirming that bond markets
also price more AI growth. In the downside, self-reinforcing yield–fiscal loop can
take hold and force central banks to stabilize bond markets.

o The AI capex cycle risk. We think that AI capex decelerates slightly to peak at
USD1trn in 2028, with value creation shifting from infrastructure build-out toward
adoption and productivity. This rests on the assumption that infrastructure and
power bottlenecks stay manageable, financing conditions don‘t tighten sharply
and AI earnings keep validating the spend. A sharper investment retrenchment,
given how much of US and Asian growth and equity performance now runs

### Page 4

Allianz Research
4
• Our scenario in numbers: Global GDP growth slows from +3.0% in 2025 to +2.6%
in 2026, recovers to +2.8% in 2027, and eases again to +2.6% in 2028. US growth
moderates from +2.1% (2026) to +1.8% (2028); the Eurozone recovers from +1.0% to
+1.3%; Asia stays the main engine at around +4.4%. We forecast Brent to average 87
USD/bbl in Q4 2026 before easing to 76 USD/bbl on average in 2027 as Gulf export
flows gradually normalize both in and around Hormuz but refined-product markets

are running out of buffers, and prices should remain elevated for the next few quarters.
European gas prices should stay elevated through year-end, with TTF averaging around
EUR65/MWh in Q4 2026, before easing to about EUR36/MWh in 2027. Inflation stays
1–2pps above target in Europe and the US until H2 2027, which is why we still expect
one more Fed hike to 4.00–4.25% and two more ECB hikes to 3.00% by year-end, with
cutting cycles starting only once inflation is back at target – earliest end-2027 – and

terminal rates of 3.50% (Fed) and 2.25% (ECB) reached in 2028.
• What it means for markets. We see bond yields dropping when markets start pricing
in our central bank scenario of a lower terminal rate in both Europe and the US.
The 10y Bund is expected to gradually drop from current levels to 3.3% at the end
of 2026 and further to 3.0% in 2028 (US 10y from 4.7% end of 2026 to 4.3% in 2028).
Credit spreads will slightly widen from current low levels given strong supply from

the technology sector. Equity markets will finish the year with double digits again but
decelerate to high single digits in 2027 and 2028, with valuations normalizing to historic
levels (S&P500 2026-2028: 14%, 8%, 8%, Eurostoxx: 15%, 9%, 8% total return). The euro
will gain gradually against the dollar, reaching 1.18 in 2028 as policy uncertainty and
a weaker economy in the US will weigh on the greenback. Private markets are entering
a more selective phase: Private equity faces a record ~13,300-company exit backlog,

### Page 5

28 September 2026
5
The summer turned three risks we had been
monitoring into part of our base case: a fresh energy
shock out of the Middle East, a bond sell-off that has
spread from the US to Japan to Europe and renewed
doubts about the AI boom. Yet the real economy has
not broken. Q2 growth surprised to the upside on net
exports, Germany‘s stimulus and robust AI-related
investment, and forward-looking indicators show limited
sign of deterioration so far, even as bond markets tell

a more troubled story, with yields at multi-year highs
and equities still reading the growth story rather than
the bond one. In our baseline scenario, global growth
slows from +3.0% in 2025 to +2.6% in 2026, recovers to
+2.8% in 2027 as the chokepoints fade and eases back to
+2.6% in 2028 as that support wanes. Within this, the US
moderates from +2.1% to +1.8%, the Eurozone recovers
from +1.0% to +1.3%, and Asia remains the main growth
engine throughout at around +4.4%. This path holds

only as long as the three chokepoints unwind rather
than escalate, and each rests on a specific, breakable
assumption. On energy, we assume the conflict stays
contained and that rerouting, demand destruction and
alternative supply let prices ease, contingent on the
Saudi pipeline outage not extending past a month; a
longer outage could push the shortfall above 8Mb/d and
Brent back near USD100/bbl, turning a contained shock
into a stagflationary one. On bonds, we assume inflation

moderates without a wage spiral and that sovereign
markets absorb elevated issuance without disorderly
repricing, a call that rests on European elections (France,
Italy) and the US midterms not adding fresh fiscal
uncertainty; if that gives way, a self-reinforcing yield-
fiscal loop could force central banks to choose between
fighting inflation and stabilizing bond markets. On
AI, we assume capex keeps supporting growth while
decelerating, peaking above USD1trn in 2028, contingent

on power and infrastructure bottlenecks staying
manageable and earnings continuing to validate the
spend; an investment retrenchment here would spill from
technology into corporate investment, consumption and
trade more broadly. Growth, inflation and rates in our
baseline hold only as long as these three assumptions
hold too.
Global outlook:
It may get better if three assumptions hold

If our assumptions do not hold, we will stumble into

one or several downside scenarios ranging from
stagflation to demand-side deflationary recessions.
Table 2 sets out what happens if our assumptions
on chokepoints break. Should Middle East de-
escalation fail, the shock persists rather than fades:
oil stays above USD100/bbl into 2027 and the picture
turns stagflationary, with US and Eurozone growth
undershooting the baseline. The Eurozone would see
a recession, while inflation runs hotter and keeps rising

through 2027, forcing the Fed and ECB to hike further
to 5.00% and 4.00%, respectively. Long yields spike
first before reversing sharply lower once the growth
damage bites, and risk assets sell off hard through 2026
– equities barely positive, credit and sovereign spreads
wider, private equity, private debt and real estate all
negative – ahead of a partial rebound in 2027. An AI
bubble burst plays out in the opposite direction: the
shock is disinflationary, US growth stalls towards zero

by 2027 and the Fed and ECB slash rates to just 0.50%,
pulling long yields and oil sharply lower. Equities fall
hardest in the US, given its greater AI concentration,
while the damage is most acute in the areas most tied
to the capex cycle – private equity, private debt and,
notably, infrastructure, which turns negative for the only
time across the three scenarios. The third scenario is a
slower-moving chain reaction: a bond sell-off triggered
by a political shock – long yields spiking and OAT and

Global trade of goods in volume is expected to grow
at +4% y/y in 2026, driven mainly by AI, which masks
a more divergent global economy. AI-driven demand
will contribute 4.8pps to global trade, which together

with European defense spending (+0.2pp) and carryover
effects (+1.4pp) add further support. Without these
offsetting forces, the underlying picture would be
considerably weaker: The Strait of Hormuz closure is
estimated to subtract 2.1pps from trade growth, while
the ongoing trade war erases a further 0.4pp. The rest
of the global economy is contracting in trade terms, and
the +4% headline number conceals what is effectively a
two-speed world.
Global goods trade growth is volume is expected to

ease to +2.9% in both 2027 and 2028 as the forces
supporting the 2026 headline begin to fade.  AI-
driven demand will remain the largest contributor to
growth (+1pp), but at slower phase than prior years as
deceleration starts. Hormuz normalization is unlikely to
be linear, causing stock rebuilding to take longer and
extend to 2028. Defense spending remains a positive but
diminishing contributor. The trade war persists through
2027, with US tariffs continuing to disrupt established

supply chains, subtracting 0.3pp from growth. In parallel,
free-trade agreements (FTAs) signed over the past
12 months will begin generating measurable export
gains from 2027, adding around 0.1pp annually, with
beneficiaries concentrated in the EU, India, Mercosur, the
UAE and Australia.
At a country level, the clearest winners are economies
embedded in AI supply chains, concentrated in
Asia-Pacific, while structurally the US trade war
unleashes the so-called “China shock 2.0” amplifying

China’s presence through global supply chains .
South Korea and Taiwan are posting export growth
rates of +10.5% and +30% y/y, respectively, driven by
semiconductor exports and other AI inputs. Mexico,
Vietnam and Malaysia are benefiting from supply-chain
diversification, while Israel’s technology exports are
also on the rise. In the developed world, the US and the
Netherlands stand out. The structural consequence of US
tariffs, combined with China‘s weak domestic demand,

are amplifying China’s role in the global supply chain
under the „China shock 2.0.“ Unable to sell as much into

### Page 9

28 September 2026
9
the US, China is redirecting exports through the global
south and into Europe at record volumes, causing Europe
to be squeezed from both sides: more Chinese imports
competing with domestic industry, and fewer US export
opportunities due to tariffs.
The effective US tariff rate currently stands at
9.2% and is expected to reach 12% following
the implementation of the second Section 301
investigation on overcapacity in October.  Export losses

are estimated at USD42bn this year, after USD74bn in
2025. We expect tariffs on China at 32%, with potential
for lower rates on non-sensitive goods and fewer
restrictions as relations are likely to remain stable in the
near term. However, the trade war is likely to intensify
after the US midterm elections as a shift in Congressional
control would place constraints on the administration‘s
domestic policy agenda and push it to rely more heavily
on trade tools. On the downside, potential risks could

push the average US tariff rate up to 21.3%. The most
likely scenarios include higher-than-expected tariffs
under the overcapacity Section 301, the opening of
a new Section 301 investigation targeting countries
accused of transshipment on behalf of China, and
continued tensions with Canada following the escalation
over the summer. While we do not expect further
developments in the near term, the threat of 50% tariffs
on Canadian autos remains while USMCA renegotiations

progress very slowly. Finally, the EU should also remain
watchful, with two main tariff risks: one linked to the
global services tax and the other tied to the new US
sanctions on Russia, allowing for tariffs on Russian
energy importers.
The global fiscal outlook continues to deteriorate at
the margin as structural spending pressures – defense
foremost – combine with the renewed cost of energy-
related support measures.  Many governments were
initially reluctant to intervene, treating the current

shock as transitory; the repeated extension of these
„temporary“ measures has instead become a recurring
drag on consolidation efforts. Looking at the regions,
US fiscal policy is unlikely to turn significantly restrictive
amid a likely divided Congress (or a Democratic
sweep) after the November midterm elections. In
this environment, we would expect only limited fiscal
consolidation in 2027 and 2028, to the tune of 0.5%
of GDP in 2027 and 0.6% in 2028 of structural deficit

improvement, relying on a mix of discretionary and social
spending restraint and „stealth“ taxation increases. In
the Eurozone, political risk is an increasingly relevant
input for spread dynamics: With France, Italy and
Spain all facing elections in 2027, pre-electoral tax cuts
and spending pledges raise the risk of fiscal slippage
precisely as consolidation becomes more pressing.
Moreover, the phase-out of NGEU disbursements adds
a further headwind: As grants are booked as fiscal

revenue, their decline mechanically weighs on headline
deficits for the bloc‘s largest beneficiaries (Southern
European countries in particular), compounding the loss
of investment support at a time when fiscal buffers are
already thinning.

### Page 10

Allianz Research
10
Inflation rates will remain elevated until the first half
of 2027 as indirect effects of elevated input costs filter
through core consumer prices.  Sharply higher energy
prices in 2026 have quickly pushed up fuel inflation in
most economies, with gasoline prices reaching historical
highs. Meanwhile, utility bills have started to drift up,
especially in European countries, as higher wholesale
gas prices are gradually being passed through. In Q4

2026, we expect CPI headline inflation at +2.9% in Japan,
+3.3% in the Eurozone, +3.4% in the UK and +3.5% in the
US, with upwards risks. Higher utility bills should continue
through 2027 even if wholesale gas prices start to decline
as we expect. Persistently elevated energy and input
costs should also increasingly prompt corporates to
increase their selling prices to protect their margins. This
will filter through to consumer prices, mostly from end-

2026 and into 2027. In particular, consumer food prices
should accelerate significantly from early 2027. In most
developed markets, we expect headline inflation to start
pulling down decisively only from Q2 2027, thanks to
favorable base effects on fuel inflation, but core prices
will remain sticky. However, high inflation and tighter
financial conditions will weigh on growth, which should
eventually limit ‘second round’ effects of higher prices
onto wages and corporate profits, while lower oil and

gas prices will also help pull down fuel and core prices.
In this environment, we see inflation rates normalizing
from the end of 2027 and in 2028. We expect headline
CPI inflation to reach +2.1% by Q4 2027 in the Eurozone,
+2.2% in the UK, +2.5% in the US and +1.4% in Japan.

More hikes ahead before easing cycle starts
in Q4 2027

US and Eurozone labor markets are set to cool further
into 2027 as slowing hiring meets accelerating AI
adoption. In the US, August‘s steady headline (+162,000
payrolls, 4.1% unemployment) masks a thinning hiring
pipeline: Soft hiring intentions and cooling wage growth
point to further pullback ahead, even as layoffs stay
contained. In the Eurozone, we expect unemployment

to drift only marginally above current lows (6.4%)
as vacancies keep falling and wage growth keeps
normalizing. The adjustment should keep running
primarily through hiring pace rather than job cuts,
delaying, not removing, the risk of rising unemployment.
Sectoral divergence should also widen, with
manufacturing staying under pressure from weak demand
and high energy costs while services hold up better. AI
adoption adds a further, structural drag on hiring: Firms

are increasingly able to absorb demand growth without
adding headcount, and with productivity gains from AI
still limited so far, the near-term effect shows up more in
slower hiring than in higher output, reinforcing margin
pressure into 2027. Our base case remains a gradual,
not sharp, deterioration, with buffers thinner than a year
ago, and Europe‘s main vulnerability now industrial
rather than energy-driven.

We expect the Federal Reserve to deliver one final

hike in December, before maintaining a prolonged
hold for most of 2027. As we expected in our last
economic outlook, the Fed delivered a 25bps interest
rate hike at its September meeting. With price pressures
expected to remain elevated in the near term, we now
see the Fed delivering a follow up hike at its December
meeting. With economic activity and the labor market
remaining resilient, the Fed will stay vigilant about the
risks of second-round effects. However, this hiking cycle

should be limited to two rate hikes. For a start, the Fed
policy stance is much less loose than in 2021-23, with the
real Fed interest rate slightly positive today (vs largely
negative in 2021-2023). Second, corporate pricing power
is less strong. Back in 2021-23, high and rising margins
were widespread across sectors, pointing to an economy
running too hot. In contrast, the rise in profits today is
much more concentrated in a handful of sectors (ICT,

energy, electronics). Thanks to a soft economy, easing
supply-chain strains (including for AI) and lower expected
energy costs, inflation should eventually cool down,
prompting the beginning of a Fed normalization from
December 2027. We expect the Fed policy rate to settle
in the 3.25-3.5% bound by mid-2028.
Following the European Central Bank’s (ECB) second
rate hike this year in September to 2.50%, we expect
two further 25bps increases in October and December

2026, taking the deposit rate to 3.00%. The ECB’s
focus is likely to remain on containing the second-
round effects of the Middle-East-related energy shock,
particularly as resilient economic activity provides
room for further tightening. Thereafter, we expect a
prolonged pause through 2027. While inflation is likely
to remain elevated in the near term, our assumption
of gradually easing oil and gas prices should support
a renewed disinflationary trend, with inflation moving

back towards target during the second half of 2027.
Against this backdrop, we expect the ECB to resume
easing in 2028, delivering three 25bps cuts to 2.25%,

the upper end of the estimated neutral-rate range. This
would mark a gradual normalization of monetary policy

as energy-related pressures fade, while the ECB continues
to balance inflation risks against the potential drag from
higher borrowing costs on economic activity. Meanwhile,
quantitative tightening continues at its full run-off pace of
around EUR45bn per month and is expected to continue
for the foreseeable future as the stock of holdings is still
high. At the current pace it would still take six years to
run down the remaining EUR3.2trn of assets sitting in the

PEPP and APP program. The annualized balance-sheet
reduction equals around 3.3% of GDP and by that exceeds
the Eurozone’s fiscal deficit of around 3.1%, further
reinforcing the net supply pressure on government bonds.
The Bank of England (BoE) is expected to provide two
back-to-back rate hikes in November and December
as elevated core inflation prompt a response . The BoE
has refrained from hiking interest rates so far despite
persistently above-target inflation (even prior the Iran

crisis). Admittedly, the BoE’s policy rate is already elevated
at 3.75%, and there is scant evidence of accelerating core
inflation. However, the indirect effects of elevated input
costs will start to show up soon as corporates will start
to increase their selling prices. A surprisingly resilient
economy provides further impetus for a likely acceleration
of core inflation in the months ahead. The labor market is
loose, but the unemployment rate has stabilized around

5%. In this environment, we expect the BoE to turn more
hawkish and deliver back-to-back 25bps interest rate
hikes at its November and December meetings, pushing
the Bank Rate to 4.25%. We see a beginning of a
normalization in November 2027 as inflation pulls back
and the economy softens. We expect the Bank Rate to
reach 3% by Q3 2028.
The Bank of Japan (BoJ) delivered a 25bps hike to
1.25% in September, accelerating its tightening on the
back of resilient activity and firm wage growth despite

headwinds from the Middle East conflict.  Inflationary
pressures have remained contained, in part thanks to
government subsidies on energy, with headline inflation
near target. However, the pass-through from elevated
energy prices should push underlying momentum
higher in the near term, with inflation picking up in Q4
to bring 2026 to +2.1%. The yen‘s slide to 40-year lows
over the summer prompted intervention and reinforced
the faster tightening path. We expect the next hike in

January 2027 and one additional 25bps increase in H1
2027, reaching a terminal rate of 1.75%. With the Fed
expected to ease in 2028 and Prime Minister Takaichi set
to replace two hawkish board members in mid-2027, we
anticipate a first 25bps cut in late 2028.

US growth is expected to cool off slightly through 2028
as ICT capex growth slows down. We have kept our
GDP forecasts at +2.1% for 2026 and +2.0% for 2027. The
US economy continues to see multi-speed growth, with ICT
capex growth expected to peak at around +18% in 2026,
while residential investment is going through another
recession year (-3.1% expected) and government spending
is soft (+0.6%). Household consumption has eased

significantly from 2025 (+2% expected after +2.6%) as
stretched consumers are moderating their spending. For
2027, although we expect a similar pace of GDP growth,
the drivers will likely change. While inflation is set to cool
off, we expect consumer spending to remain soft (+1.8%)
as households strive to rebuild their savings. We expect
ICT capex growth to moderate, but to keep increasing
at a strong clip (+10%). Meanwhile, elevated input costs
and tighter monetary policy should constrain overall

investment growth. However, residential investment
should stabilize at low levels (+0.8%) as housing
affordability improves at the margin. Exports growth
should moderate (from +4.1% to +2.0%) in line with the
cooling down of ICT investment worldwide. Meanwhile,
the supply-side of the US economy should continue
to remain constrained by weakening demographics
offsetting a gradual, AI-related pick-up in productivity
growth. We expect GDP growth to be soft in 2028 at

+1.8%.
The Eurozone growth outlook remains heavily
distorted by Ireland‘s outsized GDP swings but
underlying growth of the region remains solid.  Despite
accounting for only ~4% of Eurozone GDP , Ireland‘s
volatility skews the aggregate picture. Excluding Ireland,
we expect the Eurozone to post stable, decent growth
of around +1.3% over 2026–2028. Renewed geopolitical
tensions have driven a surge in energy prices, making
inflationary pressures more persistent; we have revised

our inflation forecast up to +2.9% this year and +2.5%
y/y in 2027 (from +2.7% and +2.0% in our July outlook),
before converging towards the +2% target in 2028.
European gas prices are climbing as the region races to
refill storage; the resulting macro risk looks contained
for now, but poses a threat to the activity outlook in
coming quarters, which has otherwise proved resilient
in recent months. As prices climb, European firms plan
to pass higher input costs on to consumers. Providing

some offset, Germany‘s updated outlook lends support –
defense and infrastructure stimulus is delivering near-
term tailwinds – while Southern Europe continues to
benefit from a stronger H1 and final NGEU inflows.
Developed markets:
Growth holding up amid different drivers

### Page 15

28 September 2026
15
Germany: External headwinds persist but the fiscal
boost creates resilience.  Elevated energy prices remain
a headwind for real disposable income and consumer
sentiment. The government’s fuel rebate will provide
only partial relief, meaning household consumption
should remain lackluster. However, business surveys and
trends in high-frequency industrial data suggest that
after some temporary weakness in Q3, a recovery in
capex is underway. Overall, fiscal stimulus will continue

to boost growth, offsetting structural headwinds from
the second China shock – exports may even begin to
recover cyclically. We expect GDP to grow by +1% this
year and +1.2% and +1.1%, respectively, in 2027 and
2028. Improved growth should allow for a gradual
firming up of the labor market over the next two years.
France’s GDP growth is expected to remain weak amid
external headwinds, tight macro policies and policy
uncertainty. We have maintained our GDP growth

forecast of +0.5% for 2026. Domestic demand has taken
a hit from elevated input costs and rising interest rates,
with both business and residential investment both set
to contract. The industrial sector has fared better, thanks
to strong demand in some sectors (transportation,
electronics, machinery) but high energy costs are a
growing headwind. Heading into the 2027 elections,
policy uncertainty could rise, keeping investment
subdued. Interest rates should remain elevated and

fiscal policy tight. On the positive side, consumer
spending has shown signs of improving despite higher
inflation as households seem more eager to reduce their
high savings. We expect GDP growth to accelerate only
modestly to +0.9% in 2027 and +1.1% in 2028.
Italy‘s activity resilience has surprised on the upside
in recent quarters, posting modest but steady growth
(+0.2–0.3% q/q over the last year). Private consumption
continued to support growth despite surging inflation

(which reached +3.3% y/y in August). Buoyant investment
has also been supported by the large inflow of NGEU
funds – Italy has now received 85% of the funds (out of
the EUR194bn allocated), easing earlier concerns about
administrative capacity to absorb such a large volume
of resources. On the back of a stronger H1, but cautious
of rising headwinds and the end of NGEU disbursements
in December, we have upgraded our forecast to +0.9%
this year (up from +0.7% in 2025) and expect growth to

settle around +0.8% in 2027–2028. With the fiscal deficit
confirmed at -3.1% of GDP in 2025 (primary balance at
+0.8% of GDP), the country remains under the Excessive
Deficit Procedure. Bringing the deficit below 3% could
have allowed for bigger spending in the 2027 budget,
providing a boost to the current government‘s pre-
electoral campaign.
Spain‘s growth does not seem to be slowing. We expect
GDP to grow by +2.5% in 2026, before stabilizing below

+2% in 2027–2028. Spain‘s outperformance remains a
defining story within the Eurozone; average quarterly
growth over the last six quarters has been +0.7%, versus
a Eurozone average of +0.3%, with private consumption
and services exports as the main drivers. Inflationary
pressures have reignited strongly, with price growth set
to average well above +3% this year. The labor market
has been a bright spot, although immigration-driven
regularization muddies the true picture behind the

headline job growth. On the political front, Prime Minister
Pedro Sánchez intends to run for re-election in 2027, but
continued scandal and turmoil have left the opposition PP
leading the polls, raising the risk of a snap election and a
conservative government before then.
The UK has held up well against external headwinds
but tighter financial conditions and higher inflation
should keep growth capped. We have revised up our
2026 GDP growth forecast significantly to +1.3% (+0.3pp).

Households have kept spending at a decent pace by
reducing their savings rate, while business investment has
maintained a healthy pace. The manufacturing sector
has rebounded from 2025 with noticeable improvements
in machinery and electrical equipment, while aerospace
continues to power ahead. In 2027 we expect slightly
lower GDP growth (+1.2%). Tighter financial conditions
induced by higher market interest rates and (expected)
policy rates should soften investment growth and keep

residential investment weak. We also forecast GDP
growth at +1.2% in 2028 as the UK economy remains
constrained by weak supply side (including softening
demographics).
Japan delivered strong Q2 growth at +1.4% y/y despite
geopolitical headwinds and elevated energy costs. We
forecast +0.9% GDP growth in 2026, supported by AI-
linked exports and investments, and relatively resilient
domestic demand. Fiscal policy remains expansionary
under the Takaichi administration, with multi-year

budgeting aimed at balancing stimulus with longer-term
sustainability. We expect growth to edge to +1.1% in 2027
supported by the continuation of the global AI cycle,
before moderating to +1.0% in 2028 as tighter monetary
policy and a fading fiscal impulse weigh on activity. We
expect inflation to average +2.1% in 2026, breaching the
BoJ‘s target in Q4 on Hormuz pass-through and food
inflation, before  reaching +2.0% in 2027 and 1.7% 2028.

### Page 16

Allianz Research
16
China‘s economy lost momentum in Q2, with GDP
growth decelerating to +4.3% y/y as the oil shock
from the Middle East conflict and persistently soft
household spending weighed on activity. Nonetheless,
we maintain our 2026 forecast at +4.7%, anchored by
robust export performance, while structurally elevated
savings rates and depressed consumer confidence
remain the primary drag on the domestic economy.
We forecast GDP growth at +4.5% (+0.1pp) in 2027 as

the global AI cycle continues to support the external
sector. In 2028, while the export contribution should
fade as the AI cycle matures, we expect the real estate
downturn to come to an end, gradually restoring
household confidence and marking a pivot in domestic
consumption, bringing growth to +4.3%. We forecast
inflation at +0.9% this year as inflationary pressure
should moderate into year-end. We expect a gradual
normalization thereafter, supported by two cuts

from the People‘s Bank of China (PBoC) in 2027, with
inflation rising to +1.1% in 2027 and +1.3% in 2028 as the
recovery in domestic demand progressively ends China‘s
deflationary episode.
In the Middle East, divergence continues, with Qatar,
Kuwait and Bahrain most exposed to conflict volatility,
keeping their real economies mostly at a standstill.
GDP contraction figures are high: -14% y/y for Qatar,
-10% for Kuwait and -8% for Bahrain. The latest attack on

Saudi Arabia’s pipeline brings the war back to Riyadh,
with a GDP contraction estimated at -2% y/y. Meanwhile,
the UAE and Oman remain more resilient as their Strait
of Hormuz bypasses allow for exports to continue, even
if at reduced speed. In 2027, a substantial rebound is
expected, especially for the Hormuz-exposed economies.
The renewed tightening cycle, on top of the ongoing
energy shock, is exposing fault lines across the EM
space depending on the intensity of energy imports

and exposure to the USD, causing several countries to
increase rates. Most exposed are a group of Sub-Saharan
Africa and South-Asian countries, but EMs across the
globe are exposed to the ongoing risks.
Emerging markets:
Tailwind from exports, mainly thanks to
AI and rerouting

### Page 17

28 September 2026
17
We forecast growth in APAC at +4.4% y/y (+0.1pp),
with 25% of it attributable to the AI investment cycle.
However, this masks significant divergence across
countries. While Taiwan, South Korea, Malaysia,
Thailand and Singapore are the primary beneficiaries,
with roughly 60% of their growth tied to AI-related
activity – largely channeled through exports – Indonesia
and the Philippines remain the region‘s laggards, having
undergone the most aggressive tightening cycles, given

their exposure to the Hormuz aftermath and domestic
political difficulties, headwinds now compounded
by El Niño effects on food supply and prices. On the
central bank front, we see India joining South Korea in a
tightening cycle in Q4, while we expect Indonesia and the
Philippines to remain on hold, though both central banks
will remain highly reactive to Middle East developments,
energy and food inflation and FX depreciation.
Looking ahead, we expect rates in the region to remain

unchanged until Q4 2027, when we forecast Indonesia
and the Philippines to enter an easing cycle.
In Africa, a deceleration is taking place from +4.5% y/y
in 2025 to +4% y/y in 2026, and then a  slight further
deterioration to +3.9% in 2027. The major risks include
inflation pressures from food and hydrocarbons and
global liquidity tightening. Senegal remains one of the
most exposed due to its high levels of debt that were
misreported by the former administration. It is the latest

nation to reach a deal with the IMF and is set to begin
a restructuring process with international bond holders.
Ghana, which already went through a restructuring
process in 2023-2024 following the Ukraine crisis, also
remains highly exposed to the current crisis as inflation
levels have remained elevated for several years. A
second layer of exposure linked to energy imports and
USD-exposure is identified in Kenya, and three northern
Africa nations: Egypt, Morocco and Tunisia. The largest

African economies, Nigeria and South Africa, remain
exposed to the current shocks but are relatively shielded.
Nigeria, historically a crude exporter, has benefited from
reforms that allowed for the free-float of the Naira and
is now benefiting from new oil refined exports. South
Africa is the only major African economy to have raised
rates (+25bps) and is expected to raise again during Q4
2026 due to its exposure to energy imports, as well as
its orthodox central bank aiming to reduce structural

inflation. However, recent structural reforms have also
supported resilience.

### Page 18

Allianz Research
18
CEE growth proved resilient in the first half of the
year despite weak external demand, geopolitical
tensions and the renewed energy shock, with domestic
demand remaining the main engine of activity.
Resilient labor markets, positive real wage growth and
still-solid consumption supported activity across most
economies, while investment increasingly contributed
in Poland, Czechia and Slovenia. We forecast regional
GDP growth at +2.1% in 2026, accelerating to +2.4% in

2027 and +2.7% in 2028 as external demand gradually
improves. Romania remains the clear outlier, with fiscal
consolidation, falling real wages and weak consumption
pushing the economy into recession in the first half of
the year, while large fiscal and current account deficits
add to its vulnerability. Inflation has remained relatively
contained across most of CEE, but the balance of risks
is increasingly tilted to the upside. We forecast regional

inflation at +4.3% in 2026, easing to +3.3% in 2027 and
+2.9% in 2028. Food prices have provided important
relief, declining recently across Czechia, Poland, Slovakia
and Hungary, but drought, extreme weather and
higher global food prices could reverse this trend in the
second half of the year and into 2027. Energy prices,
persistent services inflation and wage pressures remain
additional risks. Against this backdrop, we expect most
central banks to remain on hold for longer, with the risk

increasingly skewed toward renewed tightening rather
than further near term easing. In Türkiye, growth has
decelerated markedly, with GDP expanding just +2.3%
y/y in Q2 2026, as persistently high inflation, running at
+32% through Q3 and projected toward +30% by year-
end, continues to compress consumer demand, erode
trade competitiveness and squeeze corporate margins.
We forecast growth at +2.7% for 2026, with a rebound
toward +3.6% in 2027 conditional on inflation following a

convincing downward trajectory, which would allow the
Central Bank of the Republic of Türkiye (CBRT) to embark
on a more meaningful easing cycle from the current 37%
policy rate.
Across Latin America, fiscal consolidation remains a
binding constraint, limiting the scope for countercyclical
stimulus and requiring central banks to proceed
cautiously with monetary easing, balancing the
need to support activity against the risk of reigniting
inflationary expectations.  We forecast moderate GDP

growth of +2.3% in 2026, accelerating to +2.7% in 2027,
in both years trailing the global average by -0.3pp and
-0.1pp respectively, as the region continues to face
structural constraints on potential output. Brazil‘s economy
has been losing momentum through the first half of the
year, with the government recently downgrading its 2026
growth forecast, weighed down by weaker services and
industrial output even as the agricultural sector provides
partial offset. The BCB has delivered five consecutive

rate cuts, bringing the Selic to 13.75%, but above-target
inflation and persistent global price pressures are likely
to cap the pace of further easing. Mexico has effectively
concluded its easing cycle after Banxico cut to 6.5% in May
as low growth (+1.3% y/y forecast for 2026, accelerating
to 1.8% in 2027) coexists with still-elevated external risks,
particularly from trade policy uncertainty and USMCA
negotiations, which are leading to prolonged front-

loading dynamics across sectors and is bringing about a
structural shift in the goods exported to the North – from
the automotive sector to those related to the AI transition.
Central American countries, such as Costa Rica, Dominican
Republic, Guatemala and Panama remain relative
outperformers, supported by financial flows, tourism and
commodity prices, but El Niño risks could affect 2027-
2028 prospects in the area as well as in Andean countries.

Chile, Ecuador and Peru have already seen flooding and
logistics interruption. Colombia faces a more challenging
adjustment path amid tighter financial conditions and
spending-fueled GDP growth.

### Page 19

28 September 2026
19
Corporates:
From energy shock to rate shock
Corporate earnings remain resilient, but the breadth
of growth is narrowing sharply and sectors divide into
three very different clusters. Momentum is increasingly
concentrated in AI, defense and energy, while most of the
cyclical sectors remain under pressure. In the US, revenue
growth reached +15.2% y/y, the strongest pace since Q2
2022. Semiconductors have been the principal engine,

supported by the AI investment super cycle, while energy
companies have benefited from higher oil prices. Europe
has posted similarly robust headline growth at +11.2%, but
the underlying picture is even more concentrated: energy,
defense and semiconductors account for most of the
increase. Corporate performance is therefore no longer
moving broadly in sync. Instead, earnings are increasingly
bifurcating around three distinct sets of exposures. The

first is an AI-linked cluster spanning semiconductors,
software and IT services, and telecom infrastructure. These
sectors are capturing a disproportionate share of current
earnings momentum as capital expenditure continues
to flow into computing capacity, data infrastructure
and digitalization. The second is a group of traditionally
defensive sectors whose resilience increasingly depends
on the nature of their underlying risks. Pharmaceuticals
continue to benefit from patent protection, relatively

strong pricing power and less cyclical demand. Agrifood
enjoys similarly structural demand but is more exposed to
climate volatility and trade disruption. Energy, meanwhile,
is benefiting directly from elevated oil prices, providing
a near-term earnings tailwind even as geopolitical risks
increase uncertainty elsewhere in the economy. The third
cluster comprises the more traditional cyclical sectors
– autos, construction, machinery, textiles and transport

equipment. Here, pricing power is thinner, tariff exposure
is more direct and financing costs remain restrictive.
These companies are therefore absorbing more of the
downside from both weaker demand and a structurally
higher cost of capital.
The Iran conflict has moved from an energy shock
to a rate shock. The disruption around the Strait of
Hormuz has sharpened the divergence in industrial
cost structures, nowhere more visibly than in European
chemicals and other energy-intensive industries.

European producers were already operating at a
structural disadvantage, paying a premium for feedstock
and power relative to competitors in the US and
Middle East. The result is an accelerating reallocation
of production towards cost-advantaged capacity.
Higher-cost European plants that are idled today are
increasingly unlikely to return once conditions improve.
This points to a continuation of the regional reshuffling
of industrial production that began with the 2022 energy

shock, rather than a conventional cyclical downturn
that will simply reverse as demand recovers. In other
words, part of the adjustment is becoming structural.
The second transmission channel runs through interest
rates. Persistent geopolitical tensions, higher energy
prices and greater fiscal demands are reinforcing

pressure on borrowing costs just as many companies
face refinancing needs. The impact on profitability
is material. We estimate that a 50bps increase in the
average interest rate on corporate debt would reduce
the net margin of non-financial corporations from 9.0%
to 8.6% in the US and from 6.6% to 6.2% in Europe,
ceteris paribus. With a 100bps increase, margins fall
further to 8.4% and 6.0%, respectively – a compression

of roughly 0.6pp or 60bps in both regions. The burden
is not evenly distributed. Exposure to higher rates is
concentrated in capital-intensive, real-asset sectors
with large debt stocks and persistent refinancing
requirements. Under a 100bps shock, the net margin of
US utilities falls from 19% to about 17%, while interest
coverage declines from 3.1x to 2.4x. For European
utilities, margins fall more sharply, from 10% to 7%, and
coverage drops from 2.0x to 1.5x. US telecoms also see

interest coverage fall substantially, from 4.5x to 2.8x,
compared with a decline from 2.0x to 1.7x in Europe
(see Figure 4). The implication is that the next phase
of pressure on corporate margins may come less from
the initial energy shock itself than from its second-
round effects on financing conditions. Companies that
entered this period with high capital intensity, limited
pricing power and large refinancing needs are therefore
increasingly exposed to a combination of weaker

operating leverage and higher debt-service costs.
The corporate response is increasingly one of
balance-sheet and industrial-footprint adjustment
rather than conventional cyclical hedging.  That is
consistent with companies treating both the higher-
rate environment and the emerging tariff architecture
as durable features of the operating landscape rather
than temporary shocks. Automakers are shifting the
focus of margin defense away from unit growth and

towards cost reductions, platform simplification and
lifecycle revenues. Chemicals groups are accelerating
capacity rationalization and redirecting investment
towards structurally advantaged geographies rather
than waiting for the cycle to turn. Metals producers,
meanwhile, are maintaining capital discipline even
as electrification-related demand increasingly strains
available supply. These strategies have a common
objective: preserving cash generation and balance-

sheet capacity through a period in which neither
demand nor financing conditions can be assumed to
normalize quickly. The emphasis is therefore moving
from maximizing volume growth to protecting returns
on capital, liquidity and strategic flexibility. This also
exposes a growing asymmetry in how markets are
pricing corporate risk. The upside associated with
the AI-linked cluster is being capitalized immediately
through stronger earnings expectations and investment

flows. By contrast, the downside from higher energy
costs, tariffs and interest rates is concentrated in the
other two clusters – and particularly in Europe, where
structural energy disadvantages, weaker pricing power
in parts of industry and greater sensitivity to external
trade shocks compound the effect. The result is not
simply a divergence between sectors, but a widening
gap between companies able to fund structural growth
and those forced to manage structural adjustment. That

distinction is likely to matter increasingly for margins,
investment and corporate balance sheets going
forward.

### Page 21

28 September 2026
Global business insolvencies are running +6% above
year-ago levels year-to-date, extending what is now
the sixteenth consecutive quarter of y/y increases
and leaving levels across most markets well above
pre-pandemic norms. Among the largest economies,
the US leads with a +19% surge, followed by Japan
(+7%), Germany (+7%) and France (+5%), while the UK
(-5%) and China (0%) are the notable exceptions. The
strain is especially visible at the top end of the corporate

spectrum: Insolvencies among firms with turnover
above EUR50mn hit 134 in Q2 2026, the third-highest
quarterly tally on record, bringing the count for the first
half of the year to 247 cases (+13% y/y), with Western
Europe accounting for nearly two-thirds of the total and
industrial sectors such as chemicals, automotive and
machinery equipment showing the sharpest deterioration.
Insolvencies are projected to rise by at least +4% globally
for the full year 2026 (the fifth consecutive year of

increases) before a tentative stabilization in 2027. North
America is set to be the largest regional contributor, with
US filings forecast at +7% but a candidate for upside
revision given strong first-half momentum; 27 countries
are already tracking above our full-year projections.
In Western Europe, the expected +2% increase in 2026
would be followed by a modest -3% decline in 2027, but
nine out of 17 markets are forecast within the -4%/+4%
range, pointing to quasi-stable insolvency levels that

remain historically elevated. Asia will see contrasting
trends, with most countries prolonging their rise, except
Australia, Singapore and Hong Kong. The full impact of
the Middle East shock has yet to feed through corporate
income statements: higher input and shipping costs,
weak(er) consumer sentiment, elevated selling-price
expectations and higher-for-longer interest rates all point
to persisting margin pressure in the coming quarters,
in particular for medium, small and very small firms.

Highly leveraged sectors are particularly vulnerable as
debt-servicing costs compound the profitability squeeze,
suggesting greater differentiation across countries and
industries depending on their exposure to war-related
spillovers and, conversely, their ability to benefit from
global tailwinds such as AI and defense spending.

Capital markets outlook:
Cruel (bond) summer coming to an end
Capital markets had a difficult summer as the energy
shock and renewed inflation concerns pushed
government bond yields sharply upwards to multi-year
highs. Equities proved more resilient: Strong earnings and
AI-related investment kept returns positive despite rising
rates, while credit spreads remained close to historical
tights, supported by solid corporate results. In currencies,

EURUSD stayed broadly range-bound, while emerging-
market assets benefited from improving fundamentals
but now offer less spread compensation. Private markets
are becoming more selective: Private equity remains
constrained by a record exit backlog, private debt
continues to deliver attractive income despite pockets of
stress, infrastructure is benefiting from the AI buildout and
real estate faces renewed refinancing pressure. Looking
ahead, we expect the summer repricing to prove orderly

rather than the start of a broader market break. As the
energy impulse fades in our baseline outlook, we see
scope for gradually declining long-term rates, while equity
returns should moderate as valuations normalize.
The summer selloff in long-term rates had three
synchronized drivers: the feedback loop between
energy prices and policy rate expectations, firmer
economic activity and a higher term premium. The
repricing of the rate path was the strongest driver. The

term premium was the swing factor capturing supply-
demand imbalances emerging over summer in a
government bonds market that is moved at the margin
by price-sensitive, leveraged investors that react to
tighter funding conditions. Firmer economic activity on
either side of the Atlantic acted as underlying upside
trend. But we think US late-cycle dynamics will resume
in 2027 as the energy impulse fades and growth returns
to potential. Markets will price rate cuts. Long-term

rates will anticipate lower growth, bringing 10y yields
to 4.5% in 2027 and 4.3% in 2028. The US term-premium
rebuild looks largely complete and is actively contained
by Treasury operations (buybacks and active Treasury
issuance). The hawkish Fed turn should itself re-anchor
the long end by restoring inflation-fighting credibility.

The Eurozone sits earlier in the cycle. But its growth
impulse is moderate and there is no overshoot above
potential to justify holding policy above neutral for long.

We see markets pricing a reversal in mid-2027, easing
German 10y yields to 3.2% in 2027 and 3.0% in 2028. Both
curves bear-steepened through the summer. We now see
potential for further steepening, especially in the Eurozone
where the market-priced policy path is most excessively
tight. In both markets, the belly is the sweet spot on a six
to twelve month horizon. It captures some of the policy-
driven decline, stays supported by economic activity and

carries less term-premium risk than the long end. At current
levels, intermediate maturities show the best combination
of positive real returns and convexity.
Within the Eurozone the risk premium now sits in
France rather than Italy or Spain. The OAT-Bund spread
reached highest value since the Euro crisis (around
100bps) as markets began pricing a scenario in which the
new president (election in April/May 2027) will struggle
to gain a majority in the parliament, creating further

fiscal uncertainty. The near-term risk stays skewed wider,
and 130bps is reachable if political fragility deepens to
levels not yet seen. BTP spreads stay contained, but the
tightening impulse is missing as NGEU support fades, and
upcoming elections are an upside risk we would treat as
temporary. There is no systemic fragmentation risk within
EGBs as yield movement remain synchronized. Political
fragility, not market fragmentation, will be the theme of

the coming months.
EURUSD traded within a relatively narrow range of
1.14 to 1.18 during the last quarter, largely continuing
the pattern observed in previous quarters. Traditional
drivers such as the expected interest rate differential
and valuation metrics suggest that this range-bound
behavior is likely to persist. Within this range, short-
term exchange rate movements were primarily driven
by developments in the US, whereas events such as the

recent German elections had only a limited impact on
the currency pair. We expect this pattern to continue.
With the US midterm elections likely to increase political
uncertainty, we believe EURUSD could move closer to the
upper end of its recent trading range by year-end.
Equity markets: the 2026 earnings boost gives way
to normalization. Equities have held up through the
summer oil shock, leaving year-to-date total returns at
+13% in the US and +10% for Europe. Earnings did the

work: 12-month forward EPS growth runs near +20%
in the US, +16% in the EMU and above +30% in EMs.
But valuation dispersion is the widest in a decade: The
US trades at 19.4x forward earnings against a 20-year
average of 16.6x; the Eurozone at 16.4x and EM at 9.9x.
Valuations argue for selectivity rather than retreat:
Earnings should keep delivering the equity risk premium,

but with PEs above average in the US and Japan, we
expect the valuation premium to deflate there as growth
slows and macro risks rise, while Europe and EMs –

where expectations are lowest and the downside most
discounted – offer similar earnings sensitivity at a lower
price. We stay constructive on equities and expect Q4
to consolidate rather than extend the rally, with returns
converging towards the long-term equity risk premium
as multiples normalize. At year end, full-year 2026 total
returns should reach +14% for the US and +15% for the
Europe, before a more normal year in 2027 with +8% and
+9%, respectively. Double-digit EPS growth carries the

forecast (US: +32%/+16%/+16% over FY1-FY3; Eurozone:
+22%/+12%/+12%). US earnings revisions (+7.1% since
July) outrun nominal GDP only thanks to technology,
~30% of the index; ex-tech, the S&P 500 tracks the
economy, so the market carries the downside if AI capex
or monetization disappoints – growth, not inflation, is
the risk. Eurozone consensus prices +14.7% EPS growth
off +4.6% nominal GDP , but macro upgrades, lower
valuations and already negative sentiment support it,

while Japan looks hyped after a +20%. With positioning
crowded and implied volatility below average,
convertibles and hedges are compelling to retain equity
and earnings upside while tailoring the downside to
tolerance.
Credit markets have remained broadly resilient
through 2026, with spreads holding near the tight
end of their long-term range. In investment grade (IG),
upgrades continue to outnumber downgrades, though
the net upgrade trend has slowed from its 2025 peak.

Solid growth is offsetting the higher interest burden,
holding credit metrics: Interest coverage went below
its long-term median but stabilized above 6x in the
US and 8x in Europe, while net leverage remains near
cycle highs. In high yield (HY), global default rates have
stabilized, albeit at a level still above their long-run
average. The market has differentiated, reflecting tight
spreads yet above-average default rates: BB and B
credits have outperformed, supported by high carry and

fading recession risk, while CCC spreads remain elevated
and are likely to stay so as the weakest issuers face the
highest refinancing costs in a higher-for-longer rate
environment.
